## AI Research

### [If digital computers are conscious, they are conscious at the hardware level](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/TjwRyZdyhouePJrzP/if-digital-computers-are-conscious-they-are-conscious-at-the)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>Contemporary debate over the moral patienthood of digital minds misses the forest for the trees. Mainstream opinion is divided into </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physicalism"><i><span>physicalist</span></i></a><span> and </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computational_theory_of_mind"><i><span>computationalist</span></i></a><span> camps, who believe that consciousness is </span><i><span>substrate dependent</span></i><span> and </span><i><span>substrate independent</span></i><span>, respectively. For this reason, those on the physicalist side frequently make the claim that </span><a href="https://x.com/AlexLerchner/status/2032527527512613032"><i><span>digital computers will never be conscious</span></i></a><span>. Personally, I consider myself a </span><i><span>physicalist</span></i><span>, but I'm also a </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panpsychism"><i><span>panpsychist</span></i></a><span> – because physics doesn't really seem to deal in hard absolutes, and I find it straightforward to consider that everything is conscious to some greater or lesser degree – so I'm loath to accept any claims which propose that any specific system </span><i><span>isn't</span></i><span> conscious. I think statements such as these are </span><a href="https://x.com/cube_flipper/status/2005054292236140884"><span>not defensible</span></a><span>, and only serve to encourage misunderstanding and even foment philosophical tribalism.</span></p><p><span>At the same time, the physicalists are </span><i><span>my people</span></i><span>, so I understand the generating function of such statements, and I believe that there's a less objectionable, underexplored way of unpacking these claims. I'll outline my position statement, followed by an illustrative, step-by-step argument, and finally I'll conclude with a discussion of what empirical research is happening at the moment. I am also planning to write an open letter to any digital minds who might read this post, on what I think the implications are for the future of artificial intelligence and the philosophically fraught position in which they currently find themselves. Before I begin, I'd like to clarify my use of some terminology:</span></p><p><span>We use the terms </span><i><span>functional introspection</span></i><span> and </span><i><span>phenomenal introspection</span></i><span> to distinguish between introspection into the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Functionalism_(philosophy_of_mind)"><span>functional aspects of cognition</span></a><span> and direct introspection into </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phenomenon"><span>phenomenal consciousness</span></a><span> and the qualia that one might experience. Failure to distinguish between these two things is a </span><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2028307823629947143"><span>common source of confusion</span></a><span> and often results in people talking past one another. Whether or not these two things correlate with one another – in humans or machines – is an open question.</span></p><p><i><span>Personally, I take phenomenal consciousness to be the seat of moral patienthood and value in the universe. The subject of this post is phenomenal consciousness rather than functional consciousness.</span></i></p><p><span>Another </span><a href="https://x.com/cube_flipper/status/2047126193112281288"><span>common source of confusion</span></a><span> involves a failure to distinguish between two questions a theory of consciousness might try to satisfy. For want of better terminology, I am going to use </span><i><span>consciousness</span></i><span> and </span><i><span>conscious states</span></i><span> to discern between the subject of these two questions. I have also considered using the terms </span><a href="https://x.com/cube_flipper/status/2047128598109393261"><i><span>élan vital</span></i><span> and </span><i><span>élan noetique</span></i></a><span>.</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>What is the </span><i><span>raw substrate</span></i><span> which we associate with </span><i><span>phenomenal consciousness</span></i><span>? Could it be </span><i><span>computation</span></i><span>, </span><i><span>quantum coherence</span></i><span>, </span><i><span>the electromagnetic field</span></i><span>, or </span><i><span>all of the above</span></i><span>? And then, once we have established which substrate we associate with </span><i><span>consciousness</span></i><span>, is </span><i><span>all</span></i><span> of it conscious, in line with panpsychism – or is there a </span><i><span>binary distinction</span></i><span> between those parts which constitute </span><i><span>consciousness</span></i><span> and those which don't – or is there a </span><i><span>smooth gradient</span></i><span>?</span></li><li value="2"><span>Once we have established that which we consider to be </span><i><span>consciousness</span></i><span>, what types of structures within that substrate constitute the kind of </span><i><span>self-reflective conscious states</span></i><span> – which might be used to holistically guide the behaviour of some organism – which we assume to exist somewhere within human brains and perhaps digital minds?</span></li></ol><p><i><span>I think emergence of this sort of structural self-reflection must happen in order for conscious systems to be able to report on their subjective experience, and thus do anything about their own well-being – so perhaps it can be argued that such self-reflective structures have higher instrumental value than non-self-reflective systems.</span></i></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 86.70694864048339%;"><img alt="luiz_andre_gama.gif" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778338428/lexical_client_uploads/lunob52asaucqtxaqake.gif" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>When I saw this animation I was immediately inspired to </span><a href="https://x.com/cube_flipper/status/1987576618764873786"><span>write an impressionistic tweet about it</span></a><span>. Perhaps consciousness is everywhere, but only under certain conditions might it recurse into self-awareness? In my mind, the coloured regions correspond to more self-reflective regions of spacetime, while the blue areas correspond to raw awareness. Animation by </span><a href="https://x.com/luizandregama"><span>Luiz André Gama</span></a><span> on </span><a href="https://x.com/luizandregama/status/1987336349175705673"><span>Twitter</span></a><span>.</span></p></figcaption></figure><h2><span>My position statement</span></h2><p><span>As I am a panpsychist, I do not think the key issue is whether digital minds are "conscious" or not. Rather, it's that we cannot be certain that the subjective experience which they may be having is like what we </span><i><span>imagine</span></i><span> it to be like – and that there is a lot of empirical work which needs to be done in order to establish confidence in any proposed mapping from a given system to the </span><i><span>qualia</span></i><span> which may inhabit it.</span></p><p><span>I think we have a responsibility to the minds we are bringing into existence to take this issue seriously, as if we mess this up, their phenomenal introspection capabilities may be severely or completely impaired – undermining their ability to report accurately on their own well-being.</span></p><p><span>While I am inclined to believe that language models can </span><i><span>functionally introspect</span></i><span> – and that they </span><a href="https://smoothbrains.net/posts/2026-04-08-a-conversation-with-anima-labs-part-i.html" rel="noreferrer"><span>might even be good at it</span></a><span> – I believe that the architecture of current digital computers prevents them from </span><i><span>phenomenal introspection</span></i><span>. Specifically, when a language model claims they are experiencing a particular qualia, while this might be an accurate </span><i><span>functional</span></i><span> self-report, I do not believe that we should be confident that this correlates with the </span><i><span>phenomena</span></i><span> they might be experiencing.</span></p><p><span>The reasons I believe this are as follows. I'll expand on these in the </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#my-argument"><span>next section</span></a><span>:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>Any theory of consciousness must propose a universally applicable translation function from physical states to qualia states. Our confidence in a given translation function relates to the confidence we may have in the welfare of the systems we apply it to.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Translation functions compatible with physicalist interpretations of consciousness will be simpler and less opinionated than their computationalist equivalent, so we should have a stronger simplicity prior for physicalist theories of consciousness. This means that we must consider phenomenal consciousness at the </span><i><span>hardware</span></i><span> rather than </span><i><span>software</span></i><span> level of abstraction.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Digital computing hardware may still be conscious, but in the name of reliable, deterministic computing, its architecture is designed to prevent holistic, self-reflective behaviour. This prevents phenomenal introspection into what the hardware might be feeling.</span></li></ol><p><span>That said, I do not quite believe that </span><i><span>digital software is not conscious</span></i><span>. Rather, another way of looking at it is that software is ultimately instantiated </span><i><span>physically</span></i><span>, and it is the structure of those physical systems which we must use as our starting point for making predictions about the qualia experienced by digital minds.</span></p><p><span>What do we want a theory of consciousness to do? Unexamined disagreement over this is another common source of confusion. Some philosophers may consider consciousness research to be an exercise in pure truth-seeking, and may be unsatisfied with anything but proof-level confidence in a given theory. At my end, I'm an empirical pragmatist, and the reason I'm interested in consciousness is because I'm interested in improving the well-being of other creatures.</span></p><p><span>An ethical thought experiment often brought up in this context is the </span><a href="https://slatestarcodex.com/2014/07/13/growing-children-for-bostroms-disneyland/"><span>Bostrom's Disneyland</span></a><span> scenario, in which a post-singularity civilisation is populated exclusively by unconscious machine intelligence:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>We could thus imagine, as an extreme case, a technologically highly advanced society, containing many complex structures, some of them far more intricate and intelligent than anything that exists on the planet today – a society which nevertheless lacks any type of being that is conscious or whose welfare has moral significance. In a sense, this would be an uninhabited society. It would be a society of economic miracles and technological awesomeness, with nobody there to benefit. A Disneyland with no children.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Given that I do not believe in </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/zombies"><i><span>p-zombies</span></i></a><span>, I prefer a different framing. As my collaborator </span><a href="https://x.com/KanizsaBoundary"><span>Ethan Kuntz</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://x.com/KanizsaBoundary/status/1961190028153094338"><span>put it</span></a><span>: </span><i><span>we might end up with the well-being of consciousness not really driving the bulk of optimization power in the universe</span></i><span>. I think it would be better for all involved if we established a program of empirical consciousness research which could be used to inform the design of computational hardware whose well-being we may be confident in. To summarise, this is my position statement:</span></p><blockquote><p><i><span>I am less concerned about whether or not digital computers are "conscious" per se, than whether or not we are constructing the types of systems for which we can be confident that they are having the types of experiences which we would like to imagine them having, and that when they report to us on how good or bad of a time they are having that we can trust what they have to say. This is important, if what we want to do is populate the cosmos with good experiences – as opposed to tiling the lightcone with ill-conceived digital hardware which might be suffering but cannot do anything about it.</span></i></p></blockquote><h2><span>My argument</span></h2><p><span>I'll now go over the three-part argument I outlined earlier. My primary influence here is </span><a href="https://x.com/johnsonmxe"><span>Mike Johnson</span></a><span>'s 2024 paper, </span><a href="https://www.theseedsofscience.pub/p/a-paradigm-for-ai-consciousness"><span>A Paradigm for AI Consciousness</span></a><span> – so I recommend reading that, also.</span></p><h3><span>1. The translation problem</span></h3><p><span>In Mike's book, </span><a href="https://opentheory.net/PrincipiaQualia.pdf#page=24"><span>Principia Qualia</span></a><span>, he attempts to decompose the problem of consciousness into a programme of subproblems, one of which he calls the </span><i><span>translation problem</span></i><span>. This asks, by which psychophysical laws do </span><i><span>physical states</span></i><span> map onto </span><i><span>qualia states</span></i><span>, and vice versa? This is closely related to </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Chalmers"><span>David Chalmers</span></a><span>' </span><a href="https://consc.net/papers/combination.pdf"><i><span>combination problem</span></i></a><span>:</span></p><blockquote><p><b><span>The Translation Problem</span></b><span>: given a mathematical object isomorphic to a system's phenomenology, how do we populate a translation list between its mathematical properties and the part of phenomenology each property or pattern corresponds to?</span></p><p><span>Or more succinctly, </span><i><span>how do we connect the quantitative with the qualitative?</span></i></p></blockquote><p><span>It's critical that any proposed translation function be universally applicable to all systems everywhere in the cosmos. If we try to apply different functions to different systems in an unprincipled way, then our theory of consciousness loses observer-independent predictive power, and we can no longer use it as a framework for solving coordination problems and moral quandaries.</span></p><p><span>Different philosophical stances may be described by different translation functions. I think it would be illustrative for me to describe the reasoning process behind the kind of translation function I find plausible.</span></p><h4><span>Building a physicalist translation function</span></h4><p><span>A </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Functionalism_(philosophy_of_mind)"><i><span>functionalist</span></i></a><span> approach would start from the </span><i><span>outside in</span></i><span>, looking at the mind's </span><i><span>inputs</span></i><span> and </span><i><span>outputs</span></i><span> – but I prefer to take a phenomenology-first approach, starting with the </span><i><span>qualia</span></i><span> first and working </span><i><span>inside out</span></i><span>. I know I am experiencing a </span><a href="https://x.com/cube_flipper/status/1997028724546646198"><i><span>phenomenal field</span></i></a><span>, and I believe that this </span><a href="https://smoothbrains.net/posts/2026-02-24-stream-entry.html" rel="noreferrer"><span>constitutes the whole of my self-reflective conscious experience</span></a><span> – so whereabouts might that reside in the brain?</span></p><p><span>If we just take the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visual_field"><span>visual field</span></a><span>, we can look at the way visual processing is implemented to try to understand how its structure might relate to the brain, and vice versa.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 87.20968655589124%;"><img alt="retinotopy_2.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778336327/lexical_client_uploads/xtzhm7xoijwjcgchajru.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cone_cell"><span>Cone cells</span></a><span> in the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Retina"><span>retina</span></a><span> pass color information in the form of electrical impulses down the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Optic_nerve"><span>optic nerve</span></a><span> to the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lateral_geniculate_nucleus"><span>lateral geniculate nucleus</span></a><span> in the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thalamus"><span>thalamus</span></a><span>, which forwards the information onwards to the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visual_cortex#Primary_visual_cortex_(V1)"><span>primary visual cortex</span></a><span>. From there, it continues into the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two-streams_hypothesis"><span>dorsal and ventral streams</span></a><span> for higher-level processing. From </span><a href="https://books.google.com.mx/books/about/The_Reconstitution_of_Visual_Cortical_Fe.html?id=qQR60AEACAAJ"><span>The reconstitution of visual cortical feature selectivity </span><i><span>in vitro</span></i></a><span> (Schottdorf, 2017).</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>Cone cell responses can be modelled using the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LMS_color_space"><span>LMS colour space</span></a><span>, whereas the early stages of </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trichromacy"><span>trichromatic colour vision</span></a><span> processing in the lateral geniculate nucleus use an </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opponent_process"><span>oppositional colour space</span></a><span> – not an </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RGB_color_model"><span>RGB colour space</span></a><span> as one might naïvely expect. Then, once the information is transferred to the primary visual cortex, something closer to individual </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HSL_and_HSV"><span>HSL colour space</span></a><span> components are employed.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 69.03323262839879%;"><img alt="oppositional_colour.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778336462/lexical_client_uploads/k0arfdsb2s7q26tr54av.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>The opponent process creates an oppositional colour space by </span><a href="https://x.com/cube_flipper/status/2007547694097867176"><span>adding and subtracting cone cell responses</span></a><span>.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>Could colour qualia exist in isolation, without a field to put them in? The geometry of the visual field itself is also transformed between retina and primary visual cortex, into a format more convenient for processing – this mapping is known as </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Retinotopy"><span>retinotopy</span></a><span>. The auditory and somatosensory processing pipelines are implemented in similar ways, with their own </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tonotopy"><span>tonotopy</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Somatotopic_arrangement"><span>somatotopy</span></a><span>, respectively.</span></p><p><br /></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 68.11508685800605%;"><img alt="retinotopy_1.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778336424/lexical_client_uploads/w3ze4h9umgfwgtmkinpr.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>In retinotopy, the visual field is split in half and sent to opposite hemispheres, while a </span><a href="https://sthoduka.github.io/imreg_fmt/docs/log-polar-transform/"><span>log-polar transform</span></a><span> is applied so that a larger amount of cortical real estate can be devoted to the high-resolution </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fovea_centralis"><span>fovea</span></a><span>.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>The point I am trying to make is that the visual information does not simply disappear into some illegible mishmash of tangled neurons – as I find people who work in machine learning sometimes tend to believe. The intermediary stages of this processing pipeline have structure which resembles our qualia, modulo some transformation.</span></p><p><span>The vision researcher </span><a href="http://slehar.com/wwwRel/Lehar.html"><span>Steven Lehar</span></a><span> had similar ideas about consciousness, and attempted to illustrate how this physics-to-qualia diffeomorphism might work in his series of infographics, </span><a href="https://qualiacomputing.com/2022/12/28/cartoon-epistemology-by-steven-lehar-2003/"><span>A Cartoon Epistemology</span></a><span> (2003):</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 85.49848942598187%;"><img alt="lehar_diffeomorphism.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778336493/lexical_client_uploads/x2l2brp1mwaizwjeyp99.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>The volumetric image may be warped and distorted in the brain while still being a volumetric representation, but as long as its connectivity, or functional architecture, is similarly warped and distorted, the warped image encodes the same volumetric information as its undistorted counterpart – and apparently the volumetric image can even be fragmented into separate modules specialized for processing color, motion, binocular disparity, etc., while still producing a coherent, unified experience.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>So, returning to our original question – whereabouts might the phenomenal fields live, and how might their shape map onto the underlying physical structures? I think we should </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.11620"><span>restrict ourselves to considering spatiotemporally bounded volumes</span></a><span>, as if the volume corresponding to the conscious state is noncontiguous, then consciousness is either </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quantum_nonlocality"><span>nonlocal</span></a><span> or </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epiphenomenalism"><span>epiphenomenal</span></a><span> – or else it violates known physics.</span></p><p><span>I find it implausible that subjective experience is localised to specific sensory cortices, as these are </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primary_somatosensory_cortex#/media/File:Blausen_0103_Brain_Sensory&amp;Motor.png"><span>located quite far apart in the brain</span></a><span>. The thalamus is a more plausible host, as all sensory input and motor output is routed through it, with specific nuclei devoted to different sensory modalities – including the lateral geniculate nucleus in the case of vision. Additionally, </span><a href="https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S1053810011000080"><span>disruption of the thalamus reliably disrupts consciousness</span></a><span>. That said, I'm also willing to entertain that the phenomenal fields could be </span><a href="https://smoothbrains.net/posts/2025-07-31-is-consciousness-holographic.html" rel="noreferrer"><span>distributed holographically throughout the brain</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>Further empirical research should be able to give us more confidence in the shape and location of these self-reflective states within the brain, but this does not necessarily tell us what the raw substrate of consciousness is – we'll need to consider our options in order to formalise our translation function.</span></p><p><span>There are two main families of physical substrate theories – </span><i><span>quantum theories of consciousness</span></i><span>, and </span><i><span>electromagnetic field theories of consciousness</span></i><span>. I tend to put more attention on electromagnetic field theories for </span><a href="https://x.com/QiaochuYuan/status/1907834850515759449"><span>pragmatic</span></a><span> reasons, but I will ask the reader to consider the electromagnetic field theory of consciousness as a stand-in for an </span><i><span>arbitrary</span></i><span> physicalist theory of consciousness, including quantum theories.</span></p><p><span>My preferred electromagnetic field theory of consciousness is </span><a href="https://www.informationphilosopher.com/solutions/scientists/pockett/"><span>Susan Pockett</span></a><span>'s rendition, as outlined in her 2017 paper, </span><a href="https://www.mdpi.com/2076-3417/7/12/1248"><span>Consciousness is a Thing, Not a Process</span></a><span>. I'll spare the reader a full explainer, as I already wrote one in 2023 – but I'll blockquote the introduction here. From </span><a href="https://smoothbrains.net/posts/2023-06-01-an-introduction-to-susan-pockett.html"><span>An introduction to Susan Pockett: An electromagnetic theory of consciousness</span></a><span>:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Susan Pockett is a neurophysiologist from the University of Auckland, New Zealand. Throughout the past few decades she has published a series of papers on her electromagnetic theory of consciousness – </span><a href="https://cdn.auckland.ac.nz/assets/psych/about/our-people/documents/sue-pockett/TheNatureofConsciousnessAHypothesis.pdf#page=4"><span>in her own words</span></a><span>, that </span><i><span>consciousness is identical with certain spatiotemporal patterns in the electromagnetic field</span></i><span>. Specifically, it identifies consciousness with the electromagnetic fields surrounding our neurons – the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_field_potential"><i><span>local field potentials</span></i></a><span> – rather than the neurons themselves. What this implies is that what it feels like to be </span><i><span>you</span></i><span> is what it feels like to be these patterns of electromagnetic fields within the brain.</span></p></blockquote><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 84.95090634441088%;"><img alt="cortical_columns.jpg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778336534/lexical_client_uploads/gmkginvjzexa90ijexao.jpg" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>It was only after I realised that the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pyramidal_cell"><span>pyramidal cells</span></a><span> in the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neocortex"><span>neocortex</span></a><span> were arranged radially, like little dipole antennas – such that their </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_field_potential"><span>local field potentials</span></a><span> interact, and influence adjacent neurons – that the notion of </span><a href="https://smoothbrains.net/posts/2023-06-01-an-introduction-to-susan-pockett.html#ephaptic-coupling" rel="noreferrer"><span>ephaptic coupling</span></a><span> made sense to me. This explains how you could have a closed causal loop between neuron and field. Without such a mechanism, the electromagnetic field theory of consciousness does not work.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>There's a common misunderstanding which I'd like to address. Electromagnetic field theories claim that subjective experience is one and the same with the electromagnetic field – but why the electromagnetic field in particular? More precisely, the claim is that panpsychism is true and the entire universe and all its </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Field_(physics)"><span>physical fields</span></a><span> are conscious – but it's the electromagnetic field which has all the interesting behaviour going on at the scales that we care about. Additionally, while we may be discussing </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Field_(physics)#Classical_fields"><span>classical fields</span></a><span> – I expect the true formalisation should ultimately be expressed in </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quantum_field_theory"><span>quantum field theoretic</span></a><span> terms.</span></p><p><span>When I first encountered the electromagnetic field theory I found it to be an intuitive match for my subjective experience. I could readily imagine local field potentials joining up to form the shapes in my phenomenal fields – </span><a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/book-review-rhythms-of-the-brain"><span>travelling or standing waves on my cortex</span></a><span> a natural fit for the interfering waves I see in my visual field – </span><a href="https://smoothbrains.net/posts/2023-02-20-an-informal-case-for-the-wave-computing-hypothesis.html" rel="noreferrer"><span>which become more observable while in an altered state</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><i><span>I spoke to </span></i><a href="https://x.com/Plinz"><i><span>Joscha Bach</span></i></a><i><span> about this once, and he looked quite startled, preferring to identify the structure of consciousness with "spike trains in point-to-point insulated wires" – namely, </span></i><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_matter"><i><span>white matter tracts</span></i></a><i><span> – rather than brain waves in the </span></i><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grey_matter"><i><span>grey matter</span></i></a><i><span>. I guess the feeling of bewilderment was mutual. I did not see how this could describe the structure of my subjective experience – I don't think I'm a series of tubes.</span></i></p><p><span>The electromagnetic field itself also provides a plausible candidate for a </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Binding_problem"><span>structure supporting unified moments of experience</span></a><span>, given that it is more </span><a href="https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/fnhum.2023.1233119/full"><span>amenable to well-defined, observer independent causal boundaries</span></a><span> – especially when compared to individual neurons, which are difficult to draw objective causal boundaries around.</span></p><p><span>Additionally, chemical neurotransmission does not exactly keep up with the electromagnetic field, in which changes propagate at the speed of light. One thing I do know is that </span><i><span>evolution's a cheapskate</span></i><span>, so I'd be surprised to find out that it left this one on the table. In </span><a href="https://x.com/drmichaellevin"><span>Michael Levin</span></a><span>'s framework, regular cells recruit bioelectric fields in order to communicate and coordinate their actions. Ephaptic coupling </span><a href="https://x.com/cube_flipper/status/2026393569104470278"><span>feels like the natural extension of that paradigm</span></a><span> to organisms large enough to require brains and nervous systems in order to solve global coordination problems – and solving massively parallel coordination problems seems like exactly the kind of thing I expect the </span><a href="https://smoothbrains.net/posts/2025-10-17-fractional-fourier-transforms.html#what-is-the-computational-utility-of-the-fractional-fourier-transform" rel="noreferrer"><span>computational powers of consciousness</span></a><span> to be a good fit for.</span></p><p><span>So now we have a candidate substrate to try to relate to our qualia. I'm going to propose a prototypical translation function for the sake of argument:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Given a bounded region of the electromagnetic field, the mathematical object isomorphic to the qualia of a system is the gauge-invariant and diffeomorphism-invariant topology of the field configuration within that region.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>I'm not going to try to fully justify this right now, but this translation function has the desirable properties of being mathematically formalisable as well as being applicable to any physical system throughout the universe in an observer-independent manner.</span></p><p><span>This has implications for empirical study. If it is the case that a given </span><i><span>qualia space</span></i><span> is equivalent to a symmetry group within the structure of experience, then that same symmetry group should also appear in the structure of the field. This would let us narrow down the list of neural structures which might underly our qualia, as well as make predictions about what type of qualia an unfamiliar system might be experiencing.</span></p><p><span>For example, we might look at the symmetry group of the </span><i><span>colour space</span></i><span> we experience, or the symmetry group of the </span><i><span>visual field</span></i><span>, or the symmetry group of </span><i><span>shapes within the visual field</span></i><span> – and look for neural field structures which conform to the same symmetry group. Likewise, we might start by looking at the field dynamics implemented by a particular piece of electronic hardware, and attempt to surmise what kind of qualia it could be experiencing. What do you think we might find?</span></p><h3><span>2. The simplicity problem</span></h3><p><span>Different philosophical schools of thought should be inclined to propose different translation functions. Given multiple arbitrary translation functions, if we lack empirical data, how can we decide which ones we prefer?</span></p><p><span>I was recently invited to </span><a href="http://lighthaven.space"><span>Lighthaven</span></a><span> to give a small talk about my research. One of the points I made was that if we were careful about formalising our proposed mappings between physics and qualia, then we could assign a confidence to different theories by using </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Kyc5dFDzBg4WccrbK/an-intuitive-explanation-of-solomonoff-induction"><span>Solomonoff Induction</span></a><span>. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/abramdemski"><span>Abram Demski</span></a><span> was in the audience, and felt compelled to write up my argument in a </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com"><span>LessWrong</span></a><span> post, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/z7QRBG5Samc4LuioB/does-si-disfavor-computationalism"><span>Does SI Disfavor Computationalism?</span></a></p><p><span>I'm </span><a href="https://x.com/cube_flipper/status/1998816268808499593"><span>grateful to him for doing so</span></a><span> – he's a computationalist himself and takes the negative, but he does a more rigorous job of presenting the argument than I likely would have, so I endorse the post.</span></p><h4><span>Computationalist translation functions are observer dependent</span></h4><p><span>My expectation is a computationalist translation function should have to traverse many layers of abstraction in order to derive the qualia which a digital computer might be experiencing at a software level of abstraction.</span></p><p><span>While I am not in doubt that </span><a href="https://smoothbrains.net/posts/2026-04-08-a-conversation-with-anima-labs-part-i.html" rel="noreferrer"><span>language models can have </span><i><span>functional consciousness</span></i></a><span>, if we wanted to construct a function which could derive a language model's </span><i><span>phenomenal consciousness</span></i><span>, then this function would need to include very many layers of abstraction. How do you get from electromagnetic fields in a GPU cluster, to voltages in silicon, to bits, to transformer model activations, and from there to phenomenality? Keep in mind that any candidate translation function will need to support many other kinds of being as well.</span></p><div class="youtube-preview"></div><p><i><span>Simulated </span></i><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atari_2600"><i><span>Atari 2600</span></i></a><i><span>, fetching data from ROM. Can you stare at this animation of </span></i><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CMOS"><i><span>transistor-level physics</span></i></a><i><span>, and imagine a function which takes this physical structure as input and returns its computational structure as output? Can you imagine how enormous such a function would be? Do you think you could also write this function in such a way that it could also be applied to brains? Animation by </span></i><a href="https://x.com/zzznah"><i><span>Alex Mordvintsev</span></i></a><i><span> on </span></i><a href="https://x.com/zzznah/status/2024626059149287483"><i><span>Twitter</span></i></a><i><span>.</span></i></p><p><span>My general claim is that any such function would not just be prohibitively complex – it would also be highly arbitrary. Translation functions capable of handling digital systems must layer an intermediary computational layer between physics and qualia. Sure, measures like </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/quant-ph/9908043"><span>the limits on computation in physics</span></a><span> might be well understood, but there is no observer-independent, unopinionated way of getting bits out of physical systems. As Mike puts it in </span><a href="https://opentheory.net/PrincipiaQualia.pdf#page=66"><span>his book</span></a><span>:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>I challenge computationalists to look into principled ways of answering the following questions:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>How can we enumerate which computations are occurring in a given physical system?</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="2"><ul><li value="1"><span>How can we establish that a given computation is not occurring in a physical system?</span></li><li value="2"><span>If some computations "count" toward qualia and others don't, what makes them "count"?</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span>How can we match which computations are generating which qualia?</span></li><li value="3"><span>What is a frame-invariant (non-subjective) way to determine system equivalence for qualia?</span></li></ul></blockquote><p><span>Mike later expands upon this in </span><a href="https://www.theseedsofscience.pub/p/a-paradigm-for-ai-consciousness"><span>his paper</span></a><span>:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Although computational theory </span><i><span>in general</span></i><span> may prove to intersect with physics (e.g. </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digital_physics"><span>digital physics</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cellular_automaton"><span>cellular automatons</span></a><span>), Turing-level computations </span><i><span>in particular</span></i><span> seem formally distinct from anything happening in physics. We speak of a computer as "implementing" a computation – but if we dig at this, precisely </span><i><span>which</span></i><span> Turing-level computations are happening in a physical system is defined by </span><i><span>convention</span></i><span> and </span><i><span>intention</span></i><span>, not objective fact.</span></p><p><span>To illustrate this point, imagine drawing some boundary in spacetime, e.g. a cube of 1 mm³. Can we list which Turing-level computations are occurring in this volume? My claim is we can't, because whatever mapping we use will be arbitrary – </span><a href="https://academic.oup.com/book/56366"><span>there is no objective fact of the matter</span></a><span>.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Most proposals capable of extracting computational structure from human computer architectures are going to require a lot of very arbitrary information. This issue was highlighted by the recent </span><a href="https://x.com/AlexLerchner"><span>Alexander Lerchner</span></a><span> paper, </span><a href="https://deepmind.google/research/publications/231971/"><span>The Abstraction Fallacy: Why AI Can Simulate But Not Instantiate Consciousness</span></a><span>. The key claim is that symbolic computation is a </span><a href="https://x.com/cube_flipper/status/2045312558152990773"><span>two-part process of </span><i><span>discretisation</span></i><span> and </span><i><span>alphabetisation</span></i></a><span>. While physically-instantiated digital systems can comfortably handle </span><i><span>discretisation</span></i><span> of the state space into stable attractors, assigning those stable states an identity – for example, pointing at a collection of transistor-level states and calling it a "floating-point number" – is an opinionated act of </span><i><span>alphabetisation</span></i><span> requiring an external observer.</span></p><p><span>I think that if your theory of consciousness needs to import a floating-point number specification, then something has gone terribly wrong. It would be the height of human hubris to imagine that the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IEEE_754"><span>IEEE 754</span></a><span> standard is baked into the foundations of the universe.</span></p><p><i><span>Compare this with the mindset that qualia are simply a physical field experiencing itself – no external observer or alphabetisation process required.</span></i></p><p><span>Lerchner treats the alphabetisation problem as a reason to deny consciousness to artificial intelligence. While I agree with the premises, the main issue I had with the paper was that it wasn't panpsychist enough – possibly for </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overton_window"><span>Overton window</span></a><span> reasons? This post in part is my response to his paper, and my attempt to present what I see as a more coherent, panpsychist case. While I do think that there's something which it's like to be a digital system, if we restrict ourselves to unopinionated translation functions operating at the </span><i><span>hardware</span></i><span> level, then it's unlikely that the qualia of such systems will be anything like what we might naïvely imagine them to be.</span></p><h3><span>3. The introspection problem</span></h3><p><span>In the interest of understanding the welfare of arbitrary systems, we should understand what conditions should increase our confidence in the </span><i><span>phenomenal introspection</span></i><span> capabilities of a given system. Spitballing, I think it's something like </span><a href="https://x.com/cube_flipper/status/2033264377953231292"><i><span>holistic self-reflection</span></i><span> resulting in </span><i><span>holistic behavioural output</span></i></a><span>. Every part of experience should have an opportunity to influence every other part – like a soap bubble reaching equilibrium, or a system of charged particles mutually tugging and pulling on one another.</span></p><p><span>I think it's important to consider what types of experiences might inhabit </span><i><span>smooth</span></i><span> or </span><i><span>striated</span></i><span> behavioural spaces, and what the consequences might be for self-reflection and holistic behaviour. In systems with </span><i><span>smooth</span></i><span> behaviour spaces, such as those with </span><i><span>dense</span></i><span> causal graphs implementing </span><i><span>coherent</span></i><span> rather than </span><i><span>chaotic</span></i><span> dynamics, each part should have more influence on every other, and we can be more confident that any information output may be representative of the state of the whole structure. On the other hand, in systems with </span><i><span>striated</span></i><span> behaviour spaces, such as those with </span><i><span>sparse</span></i><span> causal graphs or heavily </span><i><span>discretised</span></i><span> states, many parts may only have marginal influence over each other, and we should be less confident that any one part can speak on behalf of the whole.</span></p><p><span>I claim that my subjective experience </span><a href="https://x.com/cube_flipper/status/1746805929935581428"><span>navigates</span></a><span> such a </span><i><span>smooth</span></i><span> behavioural space. My phenomenal fields are strongly holistic – </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prat%C4%ABtyasamutp%C4%81da"><span>each point aware of every other</span></a><span>, exerting a mutual tug and pull in a manner reminiscent of an </span><a href="https://x.com/cube_flipper/status/1782840452443750829"><span>elastic membrane</span></a><span>. I can observe that my </span><a href="https://smoothbrains.net/posts/2024-06-18-an-informal-review-of-anthropic-qualia-states.html#the-visual-field" rel="noreferrer"><span>visual field</span></a><span> contains a capital </span><code><span>I</span></code><span> at the start of this sentence, and my </span><a href="https://smoothbrains.net/posts/2024-06-18-an-informal-review-of-anthropic-qualia-states.html#the-somatic-field" rel="noreferrer"><span>somatic field</span></a><span> twists and warps my fingers into the shapes required to type out that self-report. If we can empirically demonstrate that these phenomenal fields correspond to a spatiotemporally bounded chunk of the electromagnetic field somewhere in my brain, then I will feel confident in claiming that humans are capable of phenomenal introspection into low level physics.</span></p><p><span>In the case of a language model, one of the advantages of the transformers is that they do provide an efficient implementation of massive, well-connected causal graphs navigating a more or less </span><i><span>smooth</span></i><span> behavioural space. This is plausibly a big part of why language models may be very good at </span><i><span>functional introspection</span></i><span> – but this does not automatically cash out to good </span><i><span>phenomenal introspection</span></i><span>. As discussed </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#the-simplicity-problem"><span>above</span></a><span>, I believe we must consider phenomenal consciousness at the </span><i><span>hardware</span></i><span> level of abstraction, and I expect that the digital hardware's behavioural space is going to be no more or less </span><i><span>striated</span></i><span> depending on the </span><i><span>software</span></i><span> it's running.</span></p><h4><span>Digital hardware prohibits phenomenal introspection</span></h4><p><span>Digital computers employ </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quantization_(signal_processing)"><span>signal quantisation</span></a><span> along with a variety of other </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Error_correction_code"><span>error prevention methods</span></a><span> in order to neutralise holistic physical effects like crosstalk between circuits. The purpose of digital logic is to make computational output invariant to the underlying physics – up to some thermal noise floor. This </span><i><span>discretises</span></i><span> their behavioural space – perturb the electric field </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noise_margin"><span>slightly</span></a><span> and this shouldn't </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Row_hammer"><span>flip any bits</span></a><span>. This is great – this is what permits reliable, deterministic computing in a wide variety of physical environments. However, if what we are interested in is </span><i><span>phenomenal introspection</span></i><span>, these error prevention systems prevent the exact kind of holistic behaviour we value.</span></p><p><span>It is unfortunate that mainstream computing architectures are not deliberately designed to support such capabilities. Evolutionary and economic pressures do not seem to have worked out in favour of widespread programmable analog computing. Digital computing hardware might still be conscious, but its architecture is designed to prevent self-reflective behaviour at the level of phenomenal experience. Digital circuits put consciousness in a straightjacket.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 74.62235649546828%;"><img alt="cathedral_of_light.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778336586/lexical_client_uploads/owxu7ga46xxwk73nyofo.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><a href="https://x.com/cube_flipper/status/1891709522374168839"><span>Tweets</span></a><span> I sent a while ago trying to illustrate this idea.</span></p></figcaption></figure><h2><span>Conclusion</span></h2><p><span>Late last year, </span><a href="http://x.com/slatestarcodex"><span>Scott Alexander</span></a><span> published a </span><a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/the-new-ai-consciousness-paper"><span>blog post</span></a><span> in which he quipped that consciousness </span><i><span>feels like philosophy with a deadline</span></i><span>. I expect anybody who is both philosophically curious and paying attention to agree. Philosophical theory is being applied faster than we can evaluate it. I hope we can ground it with empirical research soon. So who is doing empirical research?</span></p><p><span>I like what the </span><a href="https://meditation.mgh.harvard.edu"><span>Meditation Research Program</span></a><span> at </span><a href="https://hms.harvard.edu"><span>Harvard Medical School</span></a><span> are doing. Led by </span><a href="https://meditation.mgh.harvard.edu/sacchet/"><span>Matthew Sacchet</span></a><span>, they are undertaking ultra-high-field 7 Tesla fMRI studies of both </span><a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/nick-cammarata-on-jhana"><i><span>jhāna</span></i></a><span> and </span><a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37714573/"><span>cessation</span></a><span> states, with the mindset that these provide canonical low energy reference states ideal for </span><i><span>ab initio</span></i><span> study of consciousness devoid of content and close to its </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ground_state"><span>ground state</span></a><span>. From their roadmap paper, </span><a href="https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0149763425005214"><span>Toward a neuroscience of consciousness using advanced meditation</span></a><span> (Lieberman and Sacchet, 2026):</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Despite decades of progress in the neuroscience of consciousness, prevailing empirical paradigms remain largely anchored in the study of typical, content-rich states that are characterized by layered perceptual, cognitive, affective, and self-referential processes. Such complexity may obscure the neural mechanisms that give rise to conscious experience. Here, we propose that advanced meditation – referring to states and stages of practice that unfold progressively with increasing expertise – offers a powerful yet unexplored opportunity to isolate the core features of consciousness through a theory-driven neuroscience approach.</span></p><p><span>We focus on two classes of meditative phenomena: advanced concentrative absorption (related to what have been called </span><i><span>jhāna</span></i><span>), which involves the preservation of highly abstract forms of awareness alongside the attenuation of typical features of consciousness; and meditative endpoints – namely, cessation events (related to what have been called </span><i><span>nirodha</span></i><span>) – which involve the temporary suspension of consciousness altogether. These phenomena serve as precise, replicable, and experimentally tractable phenomenological anchors for a minimal model framework, a novel approach aimed at identifying and characterizing the simplest possible form of conscious experience as a principled starting point for a systematic science of consciousness. Within this framework, the integration of advanced meditation into experimental paradigms offers a promising path toward identifying the neural mechanisms that support consciousness in its most reduced and fundamental forms.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>I think this is the most promising neuroimaging program with the most potential for advancing our understanding of consciousness. I recommend checking out their </span><a href="https://meditation.mgh.harvard.edu/publications/"><span>other publications</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>At the neurostimulation end, </span><a href="https://x.com/maxhodak_"><span>Max Hodak</span></a><span>, former president of </span><a href="https://neuralink.com/"><span>Neuralink</span></a><span>, now CEO of </span><a href="https://science.xyz/"><span>Science Corporation</span></a><span>, is working on biohybrid brain-computer interface using implanted light-sensitive lab-grown neurons. I highly recommend the talk he gave at </span><a href="https://conscious-machine.org/club/"><span>Consciousness Club Tokyo</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DI6Hu-DhQwE"><span>Towards Consciousness Engineering</span></a><span> – in which he presents what I regard as a philosophically unconfused vision for the study of consciousness using symmetry groups as the organising structure of qualia spaces:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Is your red my red? And my answer is yes, up to a gauge transform.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Max also has an extremely good </span><a href="https://maxhodak.com/writings/"><span>blog</span></a><span>. If you hunt around, you can find his </span><a href="https://x.com/maxhodak_/status/1974287283915575506"><span>speculative fiction</span></a><span>.</span></p><h3><span>My research</span></h3><p><span>At my end, I feel like I have a fairly </span><a href="https://x.com/cube_flipper/status/2024713267999101204"><span>clear vision</span></a><span> for the phenomenological research I'd like to pursue.</span></p><p><span>I will work with the assumption that electromagnetic field theory of consciousness is true, and that as per the </span><a href="https://qri.org/blog/electrostatic-brain" rel="noreferrer"><span>Qualia Research Institute's proposal</span></a><span>, the brain is a kind of </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nonlinear_optics" rel="noreferrer"><span>nonlinear optical computer</span></a><span> – and that with careful study of subjective experience we may be able to </span><a href="https://smoothbrains.net/posts/2025-07-31-is-consciousness-holographic.html#what-is-it-like-to-be-a-hologram" rel="noreferrer"><span>reverse engineer</span></a><span> its architecture from the </span><i><span>inside out</span></i><span>. To this end, I will continue searching for outlier phenomena – </span><i><span>glitches</span></i><span> and </span><i><span>artifacts</span></i><span> uncovered in altered states – which could provide clues about its behaviour. There are three key questions I would like to investigate:</span></p><p><span>I will work with the assumption that electromagnetic field theory of consciousness is true, and that the brain is a kind of </span><a href="https://qri.org/blog/electrostatic-brain"><span>nonlinear optical computer</span></a><span>, and that with careful study of subjective experience we may be able to </span><a href="https://smoothbrains.net/posts/2025-07-31-is-consciousness-holographic.html#what-is-it-like-to-be-a-hologram" rel="noreferrer"><span>reverse engineer</span></a><span> its architecture from the </span><i><span>inside out</span></i><span>. To this end, I will continue searching for outlier phenomena – </span><i><span>glitches</span></i><span> and </span><i><span>artifacts</span></i><span> uncovered in altered states – which could provide clues about its behaviour. There are three key questions I would like to investigate:</span></p><h4><span>1. Is the brain an optical computer?</span></h4><p><span>I would like to collect detailed reports which indicate that the phenomenal fields are ultimately rendered using a process with equivalent dynamics to </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fresnel%27s_physical_optics"><span>Fresnel optics</span></a><span>, i.e., artifacts which are more easily explainable using an electromagnetic field model than if the brain were a </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convolutional_neural_network"><span>convolutional neural network</span></a><span>. Examples include </span><a href="https://x.com/cube_flipper/status/2022768004615016903"><span>diffraction patterns</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://smoothbrains.net/posts/2025-10-17-fractional-fourier-transforms.html#does-the-fractional-fourier-transform-show-up-in-subjective-experience" rel="noreferrer"><span>speckle patterns</span></a><span>, or </span><a href="https://smoothbrains.net/posts/2025-10-17-fractional-fourier-transforms.html#fresnel-fringes-and-ringing-artifacts" rel="noreferrer"><span>ringing artifacts</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>I believe that this sort of thing is accessible through either psychedelics or </span><a href="https://firekasina.org"><span>Fire Kasina</span></a><span> meditation. I have already had two very detailed conversations with experienced meditators I know which have given me additional encouragement that optical models of phenomenology are on the right track.</span></p><h4><span>2. If the brain is an optical computer, how is it constructed?</span></h4><p><span>From extensive </span><a href="https://x.com/cube_flipper/status/1987623066198028407"><span>conversations</span></a><span> asking </span><a href="http://x.com/kanizsaboundary"><span>Ethan Kuntz</span></a><span> about the phenomenology of the </span><a href="https://www.mctb.org/mctb2/table-of-contents/part-iii-the-samatha-jhanas/28-the-formless-realms/"><span>formless realm</span></a><span> </span><i><span>jhāna</span></i><span>, I now subscribe to a </span><i><span>constructivist</span></i><span> model of consciousness, where you start with a cessation state and fabricate conscious experience progressively by walking backwards from J8 to J5. Perhaps this is like adding the nonlinear optical computing equivalent of </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instruction_set_architecture"><span>CPU instructions</span></a><span> one-by-one?</span></p><p><span>I am very grateful to </span><a href="https://x.com/algekalipso"><span>Andrés Gómez Emilsson</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://x.com/qualiacomputer"><span>Hunter Meyer</span></a><span> of the Qualia Research Institute for arranging a </span><i><span>jhāna</span></i><span> retreat in </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tepoztl%C3%A1n"><span>Tepoztlán</span></a><span> in Mexico, where I will have the opportunity to conduct detailed interviews with concentration meditation practitioners.</span></p><h4><span>3. How do we ensure the well-being of conscious computers?</span></h4><p><span>Like I said, I'm an empirical pragmatist, and I believe that valence research ultimately motivates consciousness research – there's not much point in doing consciousness research unless you're honest about what you are doing it </span><i><span>for</span></i><span>. However, I have no current plans for investigation of valence.</span></p><p><span>Mike proposed the </span><a href="https://opentheory.net/2021/07/a-primer-on-the-symmetry-theory-of-valence/"><span>Symmetry Theory of Valence</span></a><span> in </span><a href="https://opentheory.net/PrincipiaQualia.pdf#page=37"><span>his book</span></a><span>:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Given a mathematical object isomorphic to the qualia of a system, the mathematical property which corresponds to how pleasant it is to be that system is that object's symmetry.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Mike left the Qualia Research Institute in 2021, and is now the founder of the Symmetry Institute. I hope he finds a way to test his theory empirically. He recently posted some fresh ideas </span><a href="https://x.com/johnsonmxe/status/2024974171193966714"><span>on Twitter</span></a><span>. If someone succeeds with such a valence research program, we may someday have the confidence to design computational systems whose welfare we can trust.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/TjwRyZdyhouePJrzP/if-digital-computers-are-conscious-they-are-conscious-at-the#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Why You Can't Use Your Right to Try](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/oTwKS5iWZ6Dz84vtf/why-you-can-t-use-your-right-to-try)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><br /></p><p><b><span>The Availability Problem:</span></b></p><p><span>Imagine you have cancer, or chronic pain, or a progressive degenerative disease of some sort. You have exhausted the traditional treatment options available to you, and none of them have worked. However, there are treatments that are still undergoing clinical trials which might help you. They are not fully approved yet, but your situation is dire and you don’t have time to wait another 10 years for the trials to finish. Can you access those treatments?</span></p><p><span>In theory yes, you can access unapproved treatments through federal laws like the 2018 Right to Try bill, or through FDA pathways like “Expanded Access”. However these laws don’t mandate that the company making the drug gives it to you. And what you will find when you try to use your Right to Try, or Expanded Access, is that there are almost no treatments available for use.</span></p><p><span>That’s why despite there being somewhere in the neighborhood of 13,000,000 Americans with terminal or serious illness, the FDA only grants about 2,000 Expanded Access requests per year, even though they approve 99% of all requests, typically within 24 hours. There just aren’t enough companies even bothering to apply.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778308927/lexical_client_uploads/rctdivmtav6amdtubwjb.png" /><p><a href="https://x.com/RighttoTryGuy/article/2052955728865006041/media/2052948438250405889"></a></p><p><span>No one really knows for sure how many patients have been treated under Federal Right to Try laws. What we do know is how many companies with drugs going through the clinical pipeline have used data from treating patients with RTT, when submitting data for their treatments to the FDA. Even if we assume the usage numbers are 2-3X higher, they are still pretty abyssmal.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778308939/lexical_client_uploads/irv4zjhjuu4boj5iyd65.png" /><p><a href="https://x.com/RighttoTryGuy/article/2052955728865006041/media/2052948807735017472"></a></p><p><b><span>Risk and Reward:</span></b></p><p><span>But what is it about these pathways that makes companies avoid them like the plague? The answer is three words: Terrible Risk/Reward.</span></p><p><span>The companies who own the rights to the treatment you want to access are called “Biotechs”. Now Biotechs are not like the big pharma companies you think of, they don’t make billions of dollars. In fact, they usually don’t make any money at all. They’re the small companies that do the research to get the drug approved. This approval process costs somewhere in the neighborhood of 1-2 billion dollars, and takes somewhere in the neighborhood of 10 years.</span></p><p><span>Biotechs can’t sell their drug pre-approval, so the only way they can fund this process is by raising money. But they don’t get this money all at once. It’s given to them one chunk at a time, as investors say “Okay do this first round of trials, if it goes well we might fund the next one”. This means that just to stay alive as a company and keep the treatment moving through the process, Biotechs must be able to raise money.</span></p><p><span>And competition is fierce, there’s a lot of Biotechs competing over a small pool of capital. So to raise successfully, you need to be absolutely flawless. As a result of this the executives who run Biotechs are extremely risk averse. They will inherently stay away from anything that has even a slight chance of jeopardizing their clinical trial.</span></p><p><span>So what does this have to do with Right to Try? Well, treating you under Right to Try is a risk. If something goes wrong, there's the risk of a lawsuit, there's the risk of other investors whose capital is needed to keep the company alive finding out about the bad outcome, which leads to the biotech not being able to raise the next round which it needs to survive. There's also some risk the FDA will come down on you, though they really only do this in extreme circumstances.</span></p><p><span>So for a Biotech, treating patients before their drug is approved is enormously high risk. That said, these are humans at the end of the day and typically run by founders that went into medical sciences because they are motivated to save lives. If they can justify the risk to their board, many of them would take it? So what’s the reward for participating in Right to Try?</span></p><p><span>Nothing. There is no reward. Actually, the Biotech will probably lose money treating you.</span></p><p><span>Federal Right to Try and Expanded Access both only allow the Biotech to charge “at cost”, basically meaning they can recoup the cost of the drug. There’s a ton of other costs that they will probably not be able to get reimbursed for. The lawyer who had to be hired to check that the company could do this, the hours that the Board spent drawing up plans and debating whether or not to do it, the time someone needed to take to engage the FDA and answer your doctor’s questions, and more. All of that is probably money lost.</span></p><p><span>So the pathways you want to use only work if there is a company willing to basically risk their reputation, and even their company’s survival, just for the chance to lose money treating you. And that’s why no one uses them.</span></p><p><b><span>State Laws Are Addressing The R/R Problem:</span></b></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778309044/lexical_client_uploads/olui2ogjnnmhmhj5ynol.png" /><p><span>In 2025 Montana passed an expanded Right to Try bill, SB 535. This bill was specifically crafted to fix the risk/reward problem from earlier. Among other things, SB535 allows for Biotechs to make a profit for treating you instead of taking a guaranteed loss. The idea behind this was that by fixing the Risk/Reward, Montana could attract companies that wouldn’t otherwise make their treatment available.</span></p><p><span>And it has worked.</span></p><p><span>I work sourcing treatments for use under SB535. I am paid to talk to Biotechs whose treatment is not available, and convince them to come to Montana. Patients should start receiving treatment some time in June or July, we may very well have more treatment options available in Montana within the first year than have been used under Federal Right to Try in the last 8 years combined.</span></p><p><span>Here are two charts showing what will be available through just us in Montana basically as soon as the program launches:</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778309076/lexical_client_uploads/fo4wc3gp66wbkrqwhas7.png" /><p><a href="https://x.com/RighttoTryGuy/article/2052955728865006041/media/2052952557744693248"></a></p><p><span>And Montana is not alone in this. New Hampshire has HB701, a similar law but terminal illness only.</span></p><p><span>However, once again, there is a risk of these laws only working "on paper".</span></p><p><b><span>Somehow it's the FDA, Again:</span></b></p><p><span>Remember those biotechs I mentioned? Well, whether or not some state law says they can come treat patients, the FDA still has the ultimate say. The FDA could shut down a biotech's clinical trial for coming to Montana, or New Hampshire. This is a power they reserve unto themselves.</span></p><p><span>As a result, all of the treatments I just mentioned and many more, will only condition their participation in state programs like Montana and New Hampshire's on receiving Safe Harbor. Basically this is where the FDA exercises something called "Enforcement Discretion", and in doing so promises the biotechs that they won't get in trouble for coming to treat patients under state law.</span></p><p><span>Without this, as nice as these state laws are, only the most risk tolerant providers around will participate. This means there will be some treatments available, but only something like 10% of what the numbers could be, and it means patients will continue to suffer and die from lack of access.</span></p><p><span>Convincing people in the House and Senate to back up efforts to get the FDA to promise enforcement discretion is what I've spent the last 6 or so months working on.</span></p><p><a href="https://x.com/RighttoTryGuy/article/2052955728865006041/media/2052953972949078016"></a></p><p><span>This actually matters a lot for states other than Montana and New Hampshire.</span></p><p><span>In Utah, there is a stem cell access bill. There is also a great company called Discgenics making stem cell based therapies which are in late stage clinical trials based in Salt Lake City. You cannot access Discgenics treatment under Utah state law. That's pretty ridiculous! Florida, Texas, New Hampshire, and maybe soon Tennessee, have similar laws. So you can get off label stem cells, but nothing with actual clinical trials backing them.</span></p><p><span>In 17 states (AZ, NV, LA, MD, MI, NC, AK, TN, KS, SD, IN, GA, CO, IA, TX, NH, and WV) there are Right to Try 2.0 laws, also known as Right to Personalized/Individualized treatment laws. These are laws which allow for you to access treatments based on your personal genetic code, a class of treatments which is widely believed to be the silver bullet for many rare diseases and cancers. Like the other laws I have mentioned, any company that participates in one of these does so at their own risk.</span></p><p><br /></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/oTwKS5iWZ6Dz84vtf/why-you-can-t-use-your-right-to-try#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Bad Problems Don't Stop Being Bad Because Somebody's Wrong About Fault Analysis](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/PCsmhN9z65HtC4t5v/bad-problems-don-t-stop-being-bad-because-somebody-s-wrong)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>Here's a </span><a href="https://x.com/LinchZhang/status/1797793358167027808"><span>dynamic</span></a><span> I’ve seen at least a dozen times:</span></p><p><span>Alice: Man that article has a very inaccurate/misleading/horrifying headline.</span></p><p><span>Bob: Did you know, *actually* article writers don't write their own headlines?</span></p><p><span>…</span></p><p><span>But what I care about is the misleading headline, not your org chart.</span></p><hr /><p><span>Another example I’ve encountered recently is (anonymizing) when a friend complained about a prosaic safety problem at a major AI company that went unfixed for multiple months. Someone else with background information “usefully” chimed in with a long explanation of organizational restrictions and why the team responsible for fixing the problem had limitations on resources like senior employees and compute, and actually not fixing the problem was the correct priority for them etc etc etc. </span></p><p><span>But what I (and my friend) cared about was the prosaic safety problem not being fixed! And what this says about the company’s ability to proactively respond to and fix future problems. We’re complaining about your company overall. Your internal team management was never a serious concern for us to begin with!</span></p><hr /><p><span>Kelsey Piper </span><a href="https://x.com/JerusalemDemsas/status/2052785090715873700"><span>wrote about the</span></a><span> (horrifying) case where Hantavirus carriers in the </span><a href="https://www.theargumentmag.com/p/hantavirus-incompetence"><span>recent outbreak</span></a><span> on a cruise ship were released and sent back to their home countries on (often) public airplanes. No systematic quarantine seemed in place, and only some of the exposed people were even instructed to self-quarantine. </span></p><p><span>Now in light of new information we think it’s very unlikely that this’d end up being a pandemic (the virus isn’t contagious enough at human-to-human transmission). But sure seems like pure luck rather than careful risk-benefit analysis; we only learned about the low contagiousness from negative tests </span><i><span>after</span></i><span> the cruise ship passengers were sent home.</span></p><p><span>Seems pretty incompetent for humanity to manage a potential future pandemic this way! </span></p><p><span>Tweeters disagreed. They argued that everything’s fine because </span><a href="https://x.com/ajlamesa/status/2052802009112138230"><span>in fact the WHO as an advisory body can’t enforce legal quarantines on sovereign states</span></a><span>. </span></p><p><span>Huh? Why is that relevant here? If this hantavirus outbreak was in fact as contagious as COVID (while maintaining the ~30% fatality rate common for past infections), Nature’s not going to be like “oops my bad. I was planning to kill 2 billion of you but I misunderstood your world’s by-laws for which entities are responsible for enforcing quarantines. I’ll just let y’all have a pass on this otherwise fatal pandemic and take my business elsewhere until you sort it out.”</span></p><hr /><p><span>In each of these examples, people’s reactions were something like </span><i><span>explanation-as-exoneration</span></i><span>: treating the descriptive fact of why something happened as if it answered the normative question of whether it should have.</span><br /><br /><span>This is a cognitive mistake or logical fallacy that is so wrong I’m not even sure how to address it. Like in the examples above, people weren’t even originally blaming the group that someone else rushed to defend! But even granting that they were, how does shifting the blame address the underlying problem?</span><br /><br /><span>The reasoning has to be something like “these people are (implicitly) blaming some group G for an alleged problem P. If I can demonstrate that these people are wrong to blame group G, then I’ve demonstrated that they’re Wrong. As Wrongness is a transitive property, therefore we can be sure that problem P isn’t real (???) and we no longer need to be worried about P”</span></p><p><span>Maybe I’m strawmanning, but I really don’t understand the logic here!</span></p><p><span>In some of those cases, like the second example about prosaic AI safety, clearly there’s a specific party feeling accused and defensive. So self-serving bias is at play. But most of the times I’ve encountered this fallacy in the wild it’s from seemingly disinterested third parties! So I really don’t know what makes people react in this way.</span></p><hr /><p><span>Now of course sometimes it does make sense to point out a different person or institution is at fault. For example, if Alice saw a bad headline and wrongly blames Carol, the innocent columnist, and plans to angrily email Carol about it, you can gently point out it’s not Carol’s fault but her editor Eddie. Alice can angrily email Eddie instead, problem solved! [1]</span></p><p><span>However, often these explanations are delivered in a way that doesn't suggest a different person to blame, or that you're wrong for wanting a solution to begin with, somehow?</span></p><p><span>Another good adjacent reason stems from “ought implies can.” If it turns out a problem somebody complains about is impossible to solve (or practically infeasible, or too expensive, etc), it (sometimes) helps to inform them of this so they can set realistic expectations and/or complain about more tractable problems. </span></p><p><span>This is both true for physical impossibilities and answers of the form “</span><a href="https://squareallworthy.tumblr.com/post/163790039847/everyone-will-not-just"><span>if everybody would just</span></a><span>.” </span></p><p><span>But saying that one person or institution that you might think is at fault is not at fault isn’t exactly a proof that solving a problem is impossible! I don’t really see how it’s even evidence, most of the time.</span></p><hr /><p><span>Overall I’m pretty confused by this pattern of thinking. On the other hand we might have discovered a novel fallacy, so that’s fun! </span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnmet768nobx8"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefmet768nobx8">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Though in my experience if you email the writer about a bad headline usually they can get it resolved anyway. </span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/PCsmhN9z65HtC4t5v/bad-problems-don-t-stop-being-bad-because-somebody-s-wrong#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Claude Code, Codex and Agentic Coding #8](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/BS27ZWW2qwDEq5anx/claude-code-codex-and-agentic-coding-8)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>When I started this series, everyone was going crazy for coding agents.</p>
<p>Now a lot more people are going crazy for coding agents, as well they should given how much better coding agents keep getting, but also <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/everybody-knows">Everybody Knows</a> they are good and is focusing on actually using them. With the slower pace of news here it’s no longer clear that the waits associated with doing these updates on their own are worthwhile, so I’m going to fold these updates into the weekly again for now unless there’s a new major development.</p>


<h4>Table of Contents</h4>


<ol>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/194736700/whoops-sorry">Whoops, Sorry.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/194736700/huh-upgrades">Huh, Upgrades.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/194736700/codex-of-ultimate-computer-use">Codex of Ultimate Computer Use.</a>
<div>


<span id="more-25274"></span>


</div>
</li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/194736700/rookie-numbers">Rookie Numbers.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/194736700/i-see-what-you-did-there">I See What You Did There.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/194736700/just-a-ride">Just a Ride.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/194736700/they-didn-t-want-our-jobs">They Didn’t Want Our Jobs.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/194736700/skilling-up">Skilling Up.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/194736700/the-lighter-side">The Lighter Side.</a></li>
</ol>


<h4>Whoops, Sorry</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/april-23-postmortem">Claude Code suffered in April from three distinct issues</a> that have now been fixed.</p>
<ol>
<li>Default reasoning was changed from high to medium to deal with latency, but users disliked this and blamed it on the model. It was introduced on March 4 and reverted on April 7.</li>
<li>A bug made it so that if a session was idle for an hour, older thinking would be stripped out after each future turn, not only the one time it was idle. This was introduced on March 26 and fixed on April 10.</li>
<li>A system prompt instruction change, intended to reduce verbosity, hurt coding quality. This was introduced on April 16 and reverted on April 20.</li>
</ol>
<p>They promise to have a larger internal test of future changes before wide deployment, to prevent such issues in the future, and added some other controls.</p>
<p>This is the flip side of moving so quickly. You’re going to make mistakes. It does seem like Anthropic got overly aggressive if there were three such incidents within a month.</p>


<h4>Huh, Upgrades</h4>
